I guess I’m here because this elevator thing has started taking over more of my day than I want to admit. I mean, I’ve been planning around not using them, like at home I’ll take five flights of stairs instead, every time, even if I’m tired or carrying stuff.

And it’s not just that—I turned down a workshop recently because it was in a high-rise, and I just kept thinking about the elevator situation instead of the workshop itself. I also stopped visiting one of my older computer students, which I feel bad about, because his building doesn’t really have another option besides the elevator. So… yeah, it’s starting to interfere with normal things, and I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. Even just thinking about using one can set me off a bit. It’s strange because logically I know, like, an elevator in an office building is probably safe. I’m not walking around thinking every modern elevator is actually dangerous or anything.

But when I’m standing in front of those older elevator doors—like the ones in my apartment building, or sometimes at work—it’s like my brain doesn’t care what I know. It immediately jumps to, “What if I get trapped in there?” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, like obviously people use them all day and nothing happens, but in the moment it feels very real. Part of me is aware I’m probably overreacting, but another part of me is already treating it like I’m about to step into something unsafe.

I don’t think so, at least not in the same way. Like, I drive and ride in cars without really thinking about it. I mean, if traffic is bad or someone’s driving aggressively, I might be annoyed or tense, but it’s not that same trapped feeling.

So… yeah, it’s starting to interfere with normal things, and I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about using an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even just thinking about using one can set me off a bit. It’s strange because logically I know, like, an elevator in an office building is probably safe. I’m not walking around thinking every modern elevator is actually dangerous or anything.\n\nBut when I’m standing in front of those older elevator doors—like the ones in my apartment building, or sometimes at work—it’s like my brain doesn’t care what I know. It immediately jumps to, “What if I get trapped in there?” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, like obviously people use them all day and nothing happens, but in the moment it feels very real. Part of me is aware I’m probably overreacting, but another part of me is already treating it like I’m about to step into something unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety or fear in situations other than using elevators?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can get nervous about normal stuff like anyone—work things, maybe meeting new people, that kind of thing—but it doesn’t feel like this. The elevator thing is very specific. It’s really the idea of being inside one, especially if it’s older or feels enclosed, and not being able to just get out.\n\nI’ve tried to think if there’s some bigger pattern, like small spaces in general or heights or crowds or something, but I don’t think so. I don’t walk around feeling scared of everything. It’s mainly elevators that seem to flip that switch for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in other small or enclosed spaces, like cars or tunnels?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, at least not in the same way. Like, I drive and ride in cars without really thinking about it. I mean, if traffic is bad or someone’s driving aggressively, I might be annoyed or tense, but it’s not that same trapped feeling.\n\nTunnels… I guess I don’t love being in a long tunnel, but I wouldn’t plan my day around avoiding one or anything. It doesn’t really stick in my mind afterward. With elevators, I’ll be thinking about it before I even get there, like where the stairs are, how many floors, whether I can make an excuse. With cars or tunnels, I don’t really do that.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed that it’s small spaces in general. It seems pretty specifically tied to elevators.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, there are definitely days I don’t think about them much at all. If I’m working from home or I’m just not going anywhere that involves an elevator, it’s not like it’s constantly in my head.

But once I know I might have to use one, especially at my apartment or at work with those older doors, then it pops up pretty fast. I’ll tell myself, like, this is probably safe, people use these all the time, it’s an office building or an apartment building, not some broken-down thing. But then I see the doors, and my mind just kind of treats it like, “Okay, you’re going to get trapped in there,” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds over the top when I’m standing here saying it, because part of me does know it’s probably fine. It’s just that in the moment, it doesn’t feel fine.

But then I see the doors, and my mind just kind of treats it like, “Okay, you’re going to get trapped in there,” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds over the top when I’m standing here saying it, because part of me does know it’s probably fine. No, I haven’t always been afraid of them. It’s more recent than that. I’d say it started maybe around seven months ago, give or take.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s not every single day, no. There are plenty of days where it doesn’t really interfere, especially if I’m just going to places where I can take stairs easily or I’m not dealing with taller buildings.

But it has started affecting actual things, not just me feeling nervous. Like at work, I turned down this workshop assignment because it was in a high-rise building, and I knew I’d probably have to deal with the elevator situation. I kind of framed it like scheduling or fit or whatever, but honestly the elevator was a big reason.

And with volunteering, I used to help this older student with computer stuff, like basic computer skills, and I stopped going to see him because his building basically requires using the elevator. I mean, technically maybe there are stairs, but realistically for where he lives and the way the building is set up, it felt like I couldn’t avoid it. So I just… stopped visiting, which I don’t feel great about.

So no, it’s not ruining every day from morning to night, but when elevators are part of the plan, it can change what I agree to do.

But then I see the doors, and my mind just kind of treats it like, “Okay, you’re going to get trapped in there,” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds over the top when I’m standing here saying it, because part of me does know it’s probably fine. It’s just that in the moment, it doesn’t feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been afraid of elevators or is this something that started at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t always been afraid of them. It’s more recent than that. I’d say it started maybe around seven months ago, give or take.\n\nIt was shortly after I moved into my apartment—the one on the fifth floor—and there was this elevator scare that kind of set it off. Before that, I mean, I didn’t especially love elevators or anything, but I used them like a normal person. I didn’t really think about it much. Then after that happened, it’s like my brain started treating elevators differently, especially the one in my building. Since then it’s just sort of stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of elevators affect your daily plans and activities every day, or are there days when it doesn't interfere at all?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single day, no. There are plenty of days where it doesn’t really interfere, especially if I’m just going to places where I can take stairs easily or I’m not dealing with taller buildings.\n\nBut it has started affecting actual things, not just me feeling nervous. Like at work, I turned down this workshop assignment because it was in a high-rise building, and I knew I’d probably have to deal with the elevator situation. I kind of framed it like scheduling or fit or whatever, but honestly the elevator was a big reason.\n\nAnd with volunteering, I used to help this older student with computer stuff, like basic computer skills, and I stopped going to see him because his building basically requires using the elevator. I mean, technically maybe there are stairs, but realistically for where he lives and the way the building is set up, it felt like I couldn’t avoid it. So I just… stopped visiting, which I don’t feel great about.\n\nSo no, it’s not ruining every day from morning to night, but when elevators are part of the plan, it can change what I agree to do.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort when you were about to use an elevator or were already inside one?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 24 / training_or_development_specialist / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response\nmanifestation: \"As soon as I step toward an elevator or hear it clunk open, my chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I feel this rush of panic before I’ve even had time to think it through.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. It can come on really suddenly. Like I’ll be fine walking through the lobby, or at least mostly fine, and then as soon as I start actually heading toward the elevator, it’s like my body reacts before I’ve even fully thought about it.

Especially if I hear it make that clunking sound when the doors open—like that heavy mechanical sound—my chest tightens right away. My hands get sweaty, and there’s this rush of panic that hits me really fast. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, I’m afraid of this,” and then slowly get nervous. It’s more immediate than that. I’ll be standing there and suddenly I feel like I need to either back away or force myself through it.

And sometimes it happens before I’m even inside. Just stepping toward it is enough. Which is frustrating, because part of me knows nothing has actually happened yet, but my body is already acting like something is wrong.

I also stopped visiting one of my older computer students, which I feel bad about, because his building doesn’t really have another option besides the elevator. So… yeah, it’s starting to interfere with normal things, and I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about using an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even just thinking about using one can set me off a bit. It’s strange because logically I know, like, an elevator in an office building is probably safe. It immediately jumps to, “What if I get trapped in there?” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, like obviously people use them all day and nothing happens, but in the moment it feels very real. Part of me is aware I’m probably overreacting, but another part of me is already treating it like I’m about to step into something unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety or fear in situations other than using elevators?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t walk around feeling scared of everything. Since then it’s just sort of stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of elevators affect your daily plans and activities every day, or are there days when it doesn't interfere at all?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single day, no. There are plenty of days where it doesn’t really interfere, especially if I’m just going to places where I can take stairs easily or I’m not dealing with taller buildings.\n\nBut it has started affecting actual things, not just me feeling nervous. Like at work, I turned down this workshop assignment because it was in a high-rise building, and I knew I’d probably have to deal with the elevator situation. I kind of framed it like scheduling or fit or whatever, but honestly the elevator was a big reason.\n\nAnd with volunteering, I used to help this older student with computer stuff, like basic computer skills, and I stopped going to see him because his building basically requires using the elevator. I mean, technically maybe there are stairs, but realistically for where he lives and the way the building is set up, it felt like I couldn’t avoid it. So I just… stopped visiting, which I don’t feel great about.\n\nSo no, it’s not ruining every day from morning to night, but when elevators are part of the plan, it can change what I agree to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort when you were about to use an elevator or were already inside one?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It can come on really suddenly. Like I’ll be fine walking through the lobby, or at least mostly fine, and then as soon as I start actually heading toward the elevator, it’s like my body reacts before I’ve even fully thought about it.\n\nEspecially if I hear it make that clunking sound when the doors open—like that heavy mechanical sound—my chest tightens right away. My hands get sweaty, and there’s this rush of panic that hits me really fast. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, I’m afraid of this,” and then slowly get nervous. It’s more immediate than that. I’ll be standing there and suddenly I feel like I need to either back away or force myself through it.\n\nAnd sometimes it happens before I’m even inside. Just stepping toward it is enough. Which is frustrating, because part of me knows nothing has actually happened yet, but my body is already acting like something is wrong.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your fear of elevators the same for all elevators, or is it worse for certain types, like older ones or those in specific locations?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 24 / training_or_development_specialist / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Specific_Phobic_Fear\nmanifestation: \"I know an elevator in an office block is probably safe, but when I look at those old doors in my apartment building or at work, my mind treats it like I’m about to get trapped or dropped, even though part of me knows that sounds over the top.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s definitely worse with certain elevators. I mean, if it’s a newer elevator in some office building, part of me can usually recognize, like, okay, this is probably inspected, people use it all day, it’s not actually dangerous. I don’t necessarily like it, but I can sometimes talk myself down a little.

But the older-looking ones are much worse, especially the ones with those heavy old doors, or the kind that make more noise when they open and close. Like in my apartment building, when I see those doors, my mind immediately goes to this image of being stuck inside, or the elevator suddenly dropping. And I know that sounds dramatic. I know, logically, an elevator in an office block or apartment building is probably safe. But when I’m standing there looking at it, especially if it looks worn or older, my brain doesn’t treat it like a normal safe thing. It treats it like I’m about to step into something that could trap me or fail somehow.

So, no, it’s not exactly equal across all elevators. Location matters too—my apartment building and sometimes work are worse, probably because I have to deal with them more and because the doors and the sounds just set off that whole thought pattern.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because this elevator thing has started taking over more of my day than I want to admit. I mean, I’ve been planning around not using them, like at home I’ll take five flights of stairs instead, every time, even if I’m tired or carrying stuff.\n\nAnd it’s not just that—I turned down a workshop recently because it was in a high-rise, and I just kept thinking about the elevator situation instead of the workshop itself. I also stopped visiting one of my older computer students, which I feel bad about, because his building doesn’t really have another option besides the elevator. So… yeah, it’s starting to interfere with normal things, and I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about using an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even just thinking about using one can set me off a bit. It’s strange because logically I know, like, an elevator in an office building is probably safe. I’m not walking around thinking every modern elevator is actually dangerous or anything.\n\nBut when I’m standing in front of those older elevator doors—like the ones in my apartment building, or sometimes at work—it’s like my brain doesn’t care what I know. It immediately jumps to, “What if I get trapped in there?” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, like obviously people use them all day and nothing happens, but in the moment it feels very real. Part of me is aware I’m probably overreacting, but another part of me is already treating it like I’m about to step into something unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety or fear in situations other than using elevators?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can get nervous about normal stuff like anyone—work things, maybe meeting new people, that kind of thing—but it doesn’t feel like this. The elevator thing is very specific. It’s really the idea of being inside one, especially if it’s older or feels enclosed, and not being able to just get out.\n\nI’ve tried to think if there’s some bigger pattern, like small spaces in general or heights or crowds or something, but I don’t think so. I don’t walk around feeling scared of everything. It’s mainly elevators that seem to flip that switch for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in other small or enclosed spaces, like cars or tunnels?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, at least not in the same way. Like, I drive and ride in cars without really thinking about it. I mean, if traffic is bad or someone’s driving aggressively, I might be annoyed or tense, but it’s not that same trapped feeling.\n\nTunnels… I guess I don’t love being in a long tunnel, but I wouldn’t plan my day around avoiding one or anything. It doesn’t really stick in my mind afterward. With elevators, I’ll be thinking about it before I even get there, like where the stairs are, how many floors, whether I can make an excuse. With cars or tunnels, I don’t really do that.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed that it’s small spaces in general. It seems pretty specifically tied to elevators.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't think about elevators or worry about using them at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, there are definitely days I don’t think about them much at all. If I’m working from home or I’m just not going anywhere that involves an elevator, it’s not like it’s constantly in my head.\n\nBut once I know I might have to use one, especially at my apartment or at work with those older doors, then it pops up pretty fast. I’ll tell myself, like, this is probably safe, people use these all the time, it’s an office building or an apartment building, not some broken-down thing. But then I see the doors, and my mind just kind of treats it like, “Okay, you’re going to get trapped in there,” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds over the top when I’m standing here saying it, because part of me does know it’s probably fine. It’s just that in the moment, it doesn’t feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been afraid of elevators or is this something that started at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t always been afraid of them. It’s more recent than that. I’d say it started maybe around seven months ago, give or take.\n\nIt was shortly after I moved into my apartment—the one on the fifth floor—and there was this elevator scare that kind of set it off. Before that, I mean, I didn’t especially love elevators or anything, but I used them like a normal person. I didn’t really think about it much. Then after that happened, it’s like my brain started treating elevators differently, especially the one in my building. Like at work, I turned down this workshop assignment because it was in a high-rise building, and I knew I’d probably have to deal with the elevator situation. I kind of framed it like scheduling or fit or whatever, but honestly the elevator was a big reason.\n\nAnd with volunteering, I used to help this older student with computer stuff, like basic computer skills, and I stopped going to see him because his building basically requires using the elevator. I mean, technically maybe there are stairs, but realistically for where he lives and the way the building is set up, it felt like I couldn’t avoid it. So I just… stopped visiting, which I don’t feel great about.\n\nSo no, it’s not ruining every day from morning to night, but when elevators are part of the plan, it can change what I agree to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort when you were about to use an elevator or were already inside one?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It can come on really suddenly. My hands get sweaty, and there’s this rush of panic that hits me really fast. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, I’m afraid of this,” and then slowly get nervous. It’s more immediate than that. I’ll be standing there and suddenly I feel like I need to either back away or force myself through it.\n\nAnd sometimes it happens before I’m even inside. Just stepping toward it is enough. Which is frustrating, because part of me knows nothing has actually happened yet, but my body is already acting like something is wrong.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators the same for all elevators, or is it worse for certain types, like older ones or those in specific locations?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worse with certain elevators. Like I’ll be fine walking through the lobby, or at least mostly fine, and then as soon as I start actually heading toward the elevator, it’s like my body reacts before I’ve even fully thought about it.\n\nEspecially if I hear it make that clunking sound when the doors open—like that heavy mechanical sound—my chest tightens right away. My hands get sweaty, and there’s this rush of panic that hits me really fast. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, I’m afraid of this,” and then slowly get nervous. It’s more immediate than that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m actually not totally sure how to explain it, because I don’t have one clear memory where I can say, “That was the exact incident and everything started right there.” I know I’ve probably described it as an elevator scare before, but when I try to pin it down, it’s kind of fuzzy.

I remember being scared in an elevator at some point, or at least having that feeling like something was wrong, but I don’t have a detailed, movie-like memory of what happened—like how long it lasted, who was there, what floor it was, or anything like that. It’s more like I remember the fear around it than the actual event, if that makes sense.

I don’t want to make up details just to give a better answer. What I do know is that elevators started feeling unsafe to me after that kind of experience, but I can’t really give you a clean story of, “the doors got stuck” or “it dropped” or anything specific like that. It’s more vague than I wish it was.

I don’t walk around feeling scared of everything. It’s just that in the moment, it doesn’t feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been afraid of elevators or is this something that started at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t always been afraid of them. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).\n  manifestation: I know an elevator in an office block is probably safe, but when I look at those old doors in my apartment building or at work, my mind treats it like I’m about to get trapped or dropped, even though part of me knows that sounds over the top.\n- key: S063\n  name: Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response\n  description: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.\n  manifestation: As soon as I step toward an elevator or hear it clunk open, my chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and I feel this rush of panic before I’ve even had time to think it through.\n- key: S064\n  name: Phobic_Avoidance_Endure\n  description: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.\n  manifestation: I’m here because I’ve started planning my days around not using elevators—taking five flights of stairs at home, turning down a workshop in a high-rise, and even stopping visits with one of my older computer students because his building doesn’t really have another option.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: It has started affecting my work and volunteering because I turned down a high-rise workshop assignment and stopped visiting a senior computer student whose building basically requires the elevator.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 7 months, since shortly after I moved into the fifth-floor apartment and had that elevator scare.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 24 / training_or_development_specialist / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S064\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort when you were about to use an elevator or were already inside one?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It can come on really suddenly. I mean, if it’s a newer elevator in some office building, part of me can usually recognize, like, okay, this is probably inspected, people use it all day, it’s not actually dangerous. I don’t necessarily like it, but I can sometimes talk myself down a little.\n\nBut the older-looking ones are much worse, especially the ones with those heavy old doors, or the kind that make more noise when they open and close. Like in my apartment building, when I see those doors, my mind immediately goes to this image of being stuck inside, or the elevator suddenly dropping. And I know that sounds dramatic. I know, logically, an elevator in an office block or apartment building is probably safe. But when I’m standing there looking at it, especially if it looks worn or older, my brain doesn’t treat it like a normal safe thing. It treats it like I’m about to step into something that could trap me or fail somehow.\n\nSo, no, it’s not exactly equal across all elevators. Location matters too—my apartment building and sometimes work are worse, probably because I have to deal with them more and because the doors and the sounds just set off that whole thought pattern.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned an elevator scare that started your fear, can you tell me more about what happened during that incident?\n\nPatient: I’m actually not totally sure how to explain it, because I don’t have one clear memory where I can say, “That was the exact incident and everything started right there.” I know I’ve probably described it as an elevator scare before, but when I try to pin it down, it’s kind of fuzzy.\n\nI remember being scared in an elevator at some point, or at least having that feeling like something was wrong, but I don’t have a detailed, movie-like memory of what happened—like how long it lasted, who was there, what floor it was, or anything like that. It’s more like I remember the fear around it than the actual event, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t want to make up details just to give a better answer. What I do know is that elevators started feeling unsafe to me after that kind of experience, but I can’t really give you a clean story of, “the doors got stuck” or “it dropped” or anything specific like that. It’s more vague than I wish it was.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs it always the sight or sound of the elevator that triggers your fear, or can it be triggered by other things as well?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mostly the sight or sound, yeah, but I guess it’s not only that. The big one is still when I’m actually stepping toward it, like once I’ve decided I’m going to use it and I’m walking up to the doors. That can set it off even before anything happens.

And the sound really does it too—when it clunks open or makes that heavy mechanical noise, my body reacts almost instantly. My chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and there’s this sudden rush of panic before I’ve even had time to think it through. It feels like my reaction is faster than my thoughts, if that makes sense.

So it’s not always just seeing it from across the lobby. Sometimes I can see an elevator and be okay-ish if I’m not going near it. But as soon as I’m moving toward it, or I hear it open, that’s when it really hits.

I mean, I’ve been planning around not using them, like at home I’ll take five flights of stairs instead, every time, even if I’m tired or carrying stuff.\n\nAnd it’s not just that—I turned down a workshop recently because it was in a high-rise, and I just kept thinking about the elevator situation instead of the workshop itself. I also stopped visiting one of my older computer students, which I feel bad about, because his building doesn’t really have another option besides the elevator. So… yeah, it’s starting to interfere with normal things, and I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about using an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even just thinking about using one can set me off a bit. It’s strange because logically I know, like, an elevator in an office building is probably safe. I’m not walking around thinking every modern elevator is actually dangerous or anything.\n\nBut when I’m standing in front of those older elevator doors—like the ones in my apartment building, or sometimes at work—it’s like my brain doesn’t care what I know. It immediately jumps to, “What if I get trapped in there?” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, like obviously people use them all day and nothing happens, but in the moment it feels very real. Part of me is aware I’m probably overreacting, but another part of me is already treating it like I’m about to step into something unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety or fear in situations other than using elevators?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can get nervous about normal stuff like anyone—work things, maybe meeting new people, that kind of thing—but it doesn’t feel like this. The elevator thing is very specific. It’s really the idea of being inside one, especially if it’s older or feels enclosed, and not being able to just get out.\n\nI’ve tried to think if there’s some bigger pattern, like small spaces in general or heights or crowds or something, but I don’t think so. I don’t walk around feeling scared of everything. It’s mainly elevators that seem to flip that switch for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in other small or enclosed spaces, like cars or tunnels?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, at least not in the same way. Like, I drive and ride in cars without really thinking about it. I mean, if traffic is bad or someone’s driving aggressively, I might be annoyed or tense, but it’s not that same trapped feeling.\n\nTunnels… I guess I don’t love being in a long tunnel, but I wouldn’t plan my day around avoiding one or anything. It doesn’t really stick in my mind afterward. With elevators, I’ll be thinking about it before I even get there, like where the stairs are, how many floors, whether I can make an excuse. With cars or tunnels, I don’t really do that.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed that it’s small spaces in general. It seems pretty specifically tied to elevators.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't think about elevators or worry about using them at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, there are definitely days I don’t think about them much at all. If I’m working from home or I’m just not going anywhere that involves an elevator, it’s not like it’s constantly in my head.\n\nBut once I know I might have to use one, especially at my apartment or at work with those older doors, then it pops up pretty fast. I’ll tell myself, like, this is probably safe, people use these all the time, it’s an office building or an apartment building, not some broken-down thing. But then I see the doors, and my mind just kind of treats it like, “Okay, you’re going to get trapped in there,” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds over the top when I’m standing here saying it, because part of me does know it’s probably fine. It’s just that in the moment, it doesn’t feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been afraid of elevators or is this something that started at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t always been afraid of them. It’s more recent than that. I’d say it started maybe around seven months ago, give or take.\n\nIt was shortly after I moved into my apartment—the one on the fifth floor—and there was this elevator scare that kind of set it off. Before that, I mean, I didn’t especially love elevators or anything, but I used them like a normal person. I didn’t really think about it much. Then after that happened, it’s like my brain started treating elevators differently, especially the one in my building. Since then it’s just sort of stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of elevators affect your daily plans and activities every day, or are there days when it doesn't interfere at all?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single day, no. There are plenty of days where it doesn’t really interfere, especially if I’m just going to places where I can take stairs easily or I’m not dealing with taller buildings.\n\nBut it has started affecting actual things, not just me feeling nervous. Like at work, I turned down this workshop assignment because it was in a high-rise building, and I knew I’d probably have to deal with the elevator situation. I kind of framed it like scheduling or fit or whatever, but honestly the elevator was a big reason.\n\nAnd with volunteering, I used to help this older student with computer stuff, like basic computer skills, and I stopped going to see him because his building basically requires using the elevator. I mean, technically maybe there are stairs, but realistically for where he lives and the way the building is set up, it felt like I couldn’t avoid it. So I just… stopped visiting, which I don’t feel great about.\n\nSo no, it’s not ruining every day from morning to night, but when elevators are part of the plan, it can change what I agree to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort when you were about to use an elevator or were already inside one?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It can come on really suddenly. Like I’ll be fine walking through the lobby, or at least mostly fine, and then as soon as I start actually heading toward the elevator, it’s like my body reacts before I’ve even fully thought about it.\n\nEspecially if I hear it make that clunking sound when the doors open—like that heavy mechanical sound—my chest tightens right away. My hands get sweaty, and there’s this rush of panic that hits me really fast. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, I’m afraid of this,” and then slowly get nervous. It’s more immediate than that. I’ll be standing there and suddenly I feel like I need to either back away or force myself through it.\n\nAnd sometimes it happens before I’m even inside. Just stepping toward it is enough. Which is frustrating, because part of me knows nothing has actually happened yet, but my body is already acting like something is wrong.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators the same for all elevators, or is it worse for certain types, like older ones or those in specific locations?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worse with certain elevators. I mean, if it’s a newer elevator in some office building, part of me can usually recognize, like, okay, this is probably inspected, people use it all day, it’s not actually dangerous. I don’t necessarily like it, but I can sometimes talk myself down a little.\n\nBut the older-looking ones are much worse, especially the ones with those heavy old doors, or the kind that make more noise when they open and close. Like in my apartment building, when I see those doors, my mind immediately goes to this image of being stuck inside, or the elevator suddenly dropping. And I know that sounds dramatic. I know, logically, an elevator in an office block or apartment building is probably safe. But when I’m standing there looking at it, especially if it looks worn or older, my brain doesn’t treat it like a normal safe thing. It treats it like I’m about to step into something that could trap me or fail somehow.\n\nSo, no, it’s not exactly equal across all elevators. Location matters too—my apartment building and sometimes work are worse, probably because I have to deal with them more and because the doors and the sounds just set off that whole thought pattern.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned an elevator scare that started your fear, can you tell me more about what happened during that incident?\n\nPatient: I’m actually not totally sure how to explain it, because I don’t have one clear memory where I can say, “That was the exact incident and everything started right there.” I know I’ve probably described it as an elevator scare before, but when I try to pin it down, it’s kind of fuzzy.\n\nI remember being scared in an elevator at some point, or at least having that feeling like something was wrong, but I don’t have a detailed, movie-like memory of what happened—like how long it lasted, who was there, what floor it was, or anything like that. It’s more like I remember the fear around it than the actual event, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t want to make up details just to give a better answer. What I do know is that elevators started feeling unsafe to me after that kind of experience, but I can’t really give you a clean story of, “the doors got stuck” or “it dropped” or anything specific like that. It’s more vague than I wish it was.\n\nDoctor: Is it always the sight or sound of the elevator that triggers your fear, or can it be triggered by other things as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the sight or sound, yeah, but I guess it’s not only that. The big one is still when I’m actually stepping toward it, like once I’ve decided I’m going to use it and I’m walking up to the doors. Sometimes I can see an elevator and be okay-ish if I’m not going near it. Since then it’s just sort of stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of elevators affect your daily plans and activities every day, or are there days when it doesn't interfere at all?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single day, no. There are plenty of days where it doesn’t really interfere, especially if I’m just going to places where I can take stairs easily or I’m not dealing with taller buildings.\n\nBut it has started affecting actual things, not just me feeling nervous. Like at work, I turned down this workshop assignment because it was in a high-rise building, and I knew I’d probably have to deal with the elevator situation. I kind of framed it like scheduling or fit or whatever, but honestly the elevator was a big reason.\n\nAnd with volunteering, I used to help this older student with computer stuff, like basic computer skills, and I stopped going to see him because his building basically requires using the elevator. I mean, technically maybe there are stairs, but realistically for where he lives and the way the building is set up, it felt like I couldn’t avoid it. So I just… stopped visiting, which I don’t feel great about.\n\nSo no, it’s not ruining every day from morning to night, but when elevators are part of the plan, it can change what I agree to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort when you were about to use an elevator or were already inside one?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It can come on really suddenly. Like I’ll be fine walking through the lobby, or at least mostly fine, and then as soon as I start actually heading toward the elevator, it’s like my body reacts before I’ve even fully thought about it.\n\nEspecially if I hear it make that clunking sound when the doors open—like that heavy mechanical sound—my chest tightens right away. My hands get sweaty, and there’s this rush of panic that hits me really fast. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, I’m afraid of this,” and then slowly get nervous. It’s more immediate than that. I’ll be standing there and suddenly I feel like I need to either back away or force myself through it.\n\nAnd sometimes it happens before I’m even inside. Just stepping toward it is enough. Which is frustrating, because part of me knows nothing has actually happened yet, but my body is already acting like something is wrong.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators the same for all elevators, or is it worse for certain types, like older ones or those in specific locations?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worse with certain elevators. I mean, if it’s a newer elevator in some office building, part of me can usually recognize, like, okay, this is probably inspected, people use it all day, it’s not actually dangerous. I don’t necessarily like it, but I can sometimes talk myself down a little.\n\nBut the older-looking ones are much worse, especially the ones with those heavy old doors, or the kind that make more noise when they open and close. Like in my apartment building, when I see those doors, my mind immediately goes to this image of being stuck inside, or the elevator suddenly dropping. And I know that sounds dramatic. I know, logically, an elevator in an office block or apartment building is probably safe. But when I’m standing there looking at it, especially if it looks worn or older, my brain doesn’t treat it like a normal safe thing. It treats it like I’m about to step into something that could trap me or fail somehow.\n\nSo, no, it’s not exactly equal across all elevators. Location matters too—my apartment building and sometimes work are worse, probably because I have to deal with them more and because the doors and the sounds just set off that whole thought pattern.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned an elevator scare that started your fear, can you tell me more about what happened during that incident?\n\nPatient: I’m actually not totally sure how to explain it, because I don’t have one clear memory where I can say, “That was the exact incident and everything started right there.” I know I’ve probably described it as an elevator scare before, but when I try to pin it down, it’s kind of fuzzy.\n\nI remember being scared in an elevator at some point, or at least having that feeling like something was wrong, but I don’t have a detailed, movie-like memory of what happened—like how long it lasted, who was there, what floor it was, or anything like that. It’s more like I remember the fear around it than the actual event, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t want to make up details just to give a better answer. What I do know is that elevators started feeling unsafe to me after that kind of experience, but I can’t really give you a clean story of, “the doors got stuck” or “it dropped” or anything specific like that. It’s more vague than I wish it was.\n\nDoctor: Is it always the sight or sound of the elevator that triggers your fear, or can it be triggered by other things as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the sight or sound, yeah, but I guess it’s not only that. The big one is still when I’m actually stepping toward it, like once I’ve decided I’m going to use it and I’m walking up to the doors. That can set it off even before anything happens.\n\nAnd the sound really does it too—when it clunks open or makes that heavy mechanical noise, my body reacts almost instantly. My chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and there’s this sudden rush of panic before I’ve even had time to think it through. It feels like my reaction is faster than my thoughts, if that makes sense.\n\nSo it’s not always just seeing it from across the lobby. Sometimes I can see an elevator and be okay-ish if I’m not going near it. But as soon as I’m moving toward it, or I hear it open, that’s when it really hits.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think it’s usually the same general things, but the intensity depends on the elevator. Like I’ve said, it’s not just seeing one exists in a building. It’s more that moment when I’m actually about to use it.

As soon as I step toward an elevator, especially if I’m close enough that I’m sort of committed to getting in, my body reacts really fast. Or if I hear it clunk open—that heavy mechanical sound—my chest tightens right away, my hands get sweaty, and I get this rush of panic before I’ve even had time to think, like, “Okay, calm down, it’s probably fine.” It just happens first, and then I’m trying to catch up mentally afterward.

So I’d say the sight and sound are the big triggers, especially the doors opening or the noises it makes, but it’s also that action of moving toward it. That’s when it stops being just an elevator in the background and starts feeling like something I have to deal with.

With friends and family, not as much directly, I guess. I mean, I don’t think I’ve had some big falling-out with anyone over it or anything like that. But it has started spilling into parts of my life where other people are involved.

The main examples I can think of are work and volunteering. At work, I turned down a workshop assignment because it was in a high-rise building, and I knew using the elevator was basically going to be part of it. I tried to make it sound practical, like scheduling or fit or whatever, but honestly the elevator was the real reason. That felt embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be able to handle things like that professionally.

And then with volunteering, I used to help a senior computer student, and I stopped visiting because their building basically requires taking the elevator. It’s not that I didn’t want to see them or help them anymore. I just kept putting it off, and then avoiding it, because getting up there felt like too much.

So I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my relationships, but it has affected responsibilities and commitments, and I feel bad because those involve people who were counting on me.

I mean, I’ve been planning around not using them, like at home I’ll take five flights of stairs instead, every time, even if I’m tired or carrying stuff.\n\nAnd it’s not just that—I turned down a workshop recently because it was in a high-rise, and I just kept thinking about the elevator situation instead of the workshop itself. I also stopped visiting one of my older computer students, which I feel bad about, because his building doesn’t really have another option besides the elevator. So… yeah, it’s starting to interfere with normal things, and I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about using an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even just thinking about using one can set me off a bit. It’s strange because logically I know, like, an elevator in an office building is probably safe. I’m not walking around thinking every modern elevator is actually dangerous or anything.\n\nBut when I’m standing in front of those older elevator doors—like the ones in my apartment building, or sometimes at work—it’s like my brain doesn’t care what I know. It immediately jumps to, “What if I get trapped in there?” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, like obviously people use them all day and nothing happens, but in the moment it feels very real. Part of me is aware I’m probably overreacting, but another part of me is already treating it like I’m about to step into something unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety or fear in situations other than using elevators?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can get nervous about normal stuff like anyone—work things, maybe meeting new people, that kind of thing—but it doesn’t feel like this. The elevator thing is very specific. It’s really the idea of being inside one, especially if it’s older or feels enclosed, and not being able to just get out.\n\nI’ve tried to think if there’s some bigger pattern, like small spaces in general or heights or crowds or something, but I don’t think so. I don’t walk around feeling scared of everything. It’s mainly elevators that seem to flip that switch for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in other small or enclosed spaces, like cars or tunnels?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, at least not in the same way. Like, I drive and ride in cars without really thinking about it. I mean, if traffic is bad or someone’s driving aggressively, I might be annoyed or tense, but it’s not that same trapped feeling.\n\nTunnels… I guess I don’t love being in a long tunnel, but I wouldn’t plan my day around avoiding one or anything. It doesn’t really stick in my mind afterward. With elevators, I’ll be thinking about it before I even get there, like where the stairs are, how many floors, whether I can make an excuse. With cars or tunnels, I don’t really do that.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed that it’s small spaces in general. It seems pretty specifically tied to elevators.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't think about elevators or worry about using them at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, there are definitely days I don’t think about them much at all. If I’m working from home or I’m just not going anywhere that involves an elevator, it’s not like it’s constantly in my head.\n\nBut once I know I might have to use one, especially at my apartment or at work with those older doors, then it pops up pretty fast. I’ll tell myself, like, this is probably safe, people use these all the time, it’s an office building or an apartment building, not some broken-down thing. But then I see the doors, and my mind just kind of treats it like, “Okay, you’re going to get trapped in there,” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds over the top when I’m standing here saying it, because part of me does know it’s probably fine. It’s just that in the moment, it doesn’t feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been afraid of elevators or is this something that started at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t always been afraid of them. It’s more recent than that. I’d say it started maybe around seven months ago, give or take.\n\nIt was shortly after I moved into my apartment—the one on the fifth floor—and there was this elevator scare that kind of set it off. Before that, I mean, I didn’t especially love elevators or anything, but I used them like a normal person. I didn’t really think about it much. Then after that happened, it’s like my brain started treating elevators differently, especially the one in my building. Since then it’s just sort of stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of elevators affect your daily plans and activities every day, or are there days when it doesn't interfere at all?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single day, no. There are plenty of days where it doesn’t really interfere, especially if I’m just going to places where I can take stairs easily or I’m not dealing with taller buildings.\n\nBut it has started affecting actual things, not just me feeling nervous. Like at work, I turned down this workshop assignment because it was in a high-rise building, and I knew I’d probably have to deal with the elevator situation. I kind of framed it like scheduling or fit or whatever, but honestly the elevator was a big reason.\n\nAnd with volunteering, I used to help this older student with computer stuff, like basic computer skills, and I stopped going to see him because his building basically requires using the elevator. I mean, technically maybe there are stairs, but realistically for where he lives and the way the building is set up, it felt like I couldn’t avoid it. So I just… stopped visiting, which I don’t feel great about.\n\nSo no, it’s not ruining every day from morning to night, but when elevators are part of the plan, it can change what I agree to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort when you were about to use an elevator or were already inside one?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It can come on really suddenly. Like I’ll be fine walking through the lobby, or at least mostly fine, and then as soon as I start actually heading toward the elevator, it’s like my body reacts before I’ve even fully thought about it.\n\nEspecially if I hear it make that clunking sound when the doors open—like that heavy mechanical sound—my chest tightens right away. My hands get sweaty, and there’s this rush of panic that hits me really fast. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, I’m afraid of this,” and then slowly get nervous. It’s more immediate than that. I’ll be standing there and suddenly I feel like I need to either back away or force myself through it.\n\nAnd sometimes it happens before I’m even inside. Just stepping toward it is enough. Which is frustrating, because part of me knows nothing has actually happened yet, but my body is already acting like something is wrong.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators the same for all elevators, or is it worse for certain types, like older ones or those in specific locations?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worse with certain elevators. I mean, if it’s a newer elevator in some office building, part of me can usually recognize, like, okay, this is probably inspected, people use it all day, it’s not actually dangerous. I don’t necessarily like it, but I can sometimes talk myself down a little.\n\nBut the older-looking ones are much worse, especially the ones with those heavy old doors, or the kind that make more noise when they open and close. Like in my apartment building, when I see those doors, my mind immediately goes to this image of being stuck inside, or the elevator suddenly dropping. And I know that sounds dramatic. I know, logically, an elevator in an office block or apartment building is probably safe. But when I’m standing there looking at it, especially if it looks worn or older, my brain doesn’t treat it like a normal safe thing. It treats it like I’m about to step into something that could trap me or fail somehow.\n\nSo, no, it’s not exactly equal across all elevators. Location matters too—my apartment building and sometimes work are worse, probably because I have to deal with them more and because the doors and the sounds just set off that whole thought pattern.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned an elevator scare that started your fear, can you tell me more about what happened during that incident?\n\nPatient: I’m actually not totally sure how to explain it, because I don’t have one clear memory where I can say, “That was the exact incident and everything started right there.” I know I’ve probably described it as an elevator scare before, but when I try to pin it down, it’s kind of fuzzy.\n\nI remember being scared in an elevator at some point, or at least having that feeling like something was wrong, but I don’t have a detailed, movie-like memory of what happened—like how long it lasted, who was there, what floor it was, or anything like that. It’s more like I remember the fear around it than the actual event, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t want to make up details just to give a better answer. What I do know is that elevators started feeling unsafe to me after that kind of experience, but I can’t really give you a clean story of, “the doors got stuck” or “it dropped” or anything specific like that. It’s more vague than I wish it was.\n\nDoctor: Is it always the sight or sound of the elevator that triggers your fear, or can it be triggered by other things as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the sight or sound, yeah, but I guess it’s not only that. The big one is still when I’m actually stepping toward it, like once I’ve decided I’m going to use it and I’m walking up to the doors. That can set it off even before anything happens.\n\nAnd the sound really does it too—when it clunks open or makes that heavy mechanical noise, my body reacts almost instantly. My chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and there’s this sudden rush of panic before I’ve even had time to think it through. It feels like my reaction is faster than my thoughts, if that makes sense.\n\nSo it’s not always just seeing it from across the lobby. Sometimes I can see an elevator and be okay-ish if I’m not going near it. But as soon as I’m moving toward it, or I hear it open, that’s when it really hits.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators always triggered by the same things, like the sight or sound of specific elevators?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s usually the same general things, but the intensity depends on the elevator. Like I’ve said, it’s not just seeing one exists in a building. It’s more that moment when I’m actually about to use it.\n\nAs soon as I step toward an elevator, especially if I’m close enough that I’m sort of committed to getting in, my body reacts really fast. Or if I hear it clunk open—that heavy mechanical sound—my chest tightens right away, my hands get sweaty, and I get this rush of panic before I’ve even had time to think, like, “Okay, calm down, it’s probably fine.” It just happens first, and then I’m trying to catch up mentally afterward.\n\nSo I’d say the sight and sound are the big triggers, especially the doors opening or the noises it makes, but it’s also that action of moving toward it. That’s when it stops being just an elevator in the background and starts feeling like something I have to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: With friends and family, not as much directly, I guess. I mean, I don’t think I’ve had some big falling-out with anyone over it or anything like that. But it has started spilling into parts of my life where other people are involved.\n\nThe main examples I can think of are work and volunteering. At work, I turned down a workshop assignment because it was in a high-rise building, and I knew using the elevator was basically going to be part of it. I tried to make it sound practical, like scheduling or fit or whatever, but honestly the elevator was the real reason. That felt embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be able to handle things like that professionally.\n\nAnd then with volunteering, I used to help a senior computer student, and I stopped visiting because their building basically requires taking the elevator. It’s not that I didn’t want to see them or help them anymore. I just kept putting it off, and then avoiding it, because getting up there felt like too much.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my relationships, but it has affected responsibilities and commitments, and I feel bad because those involve people who were counting on me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

That’s when it stops being just an elevator in the background and starts feeling like something I have to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: With friends and family, not as much directly, I guess. I mean, I don’t think I’ve had some big falling-out with anyone over it or anything like that. But it has started spilling into parts of my life where other people are involved.\n\nThe main examples I can think of are work and volunteering. At work, I turned down a workshop assignment because it was in a high-rise building, and I knew using the elevator was basically going to be part of it. I tried to make it sound practical, like scheduling or fit or whatever, but honestly the elevator was the real reason. That felt embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be able to handle things like that professionally.\n\nAnd then with volunteering, I used to help a senior computer student, and I stopped visiting because their building basically requires taking the elevator. It’s not that I didn’t want to see them or help them anymore. I just kept putting it off, and then avoiding it, because getting up there felt like too much.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my relationships, but it has affected responsibilities and commitments, and I feel bad because those involve people who were counting on me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your fear of elevators caused you to avoid social gatherings or events because they are held in buildings with elevators?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… not a lot of purely social things, like parties or dinners, but it’s definitely made me avoid things when I know elevators are involved. That’s kind of why I’m here, honestly. I’ve started planning my days around not having to use them.

And at work I turned down a workshop because it was in a high-rise, and I knew there wasn’t really a way to do it without dealing with the elevator. I didn’t really say that was the reason, but it was.

And then there’s this older computer student I used to visit to help with computer stuff, and I stopped going because his building basically doesn’t give you another option. That one makes me feel bad, because it affects someone else, not just me. So, maybe not “social gatherings” exactly, but events and visits, yes—if the building means I have to use an elevator, I’ll try to get out of it or avoid it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because this elevator thing has started taking over more of my day than I want to admit. I mean, I’ve been planning around not using them, like at home I’ll take five flights of stairs instead, every time, even if I’m tired or carrying stuff.\n\nAnd it’s not just that—I turned down a workshop recently because it was in a high-rise, and I just kept thinking about the elevator situation instead of the workshop itself. I also stopped visiting one of my older computer students, which I feel bad about, because his building doesn’t really have another option besides the elevator. So… yeah, it’s starting to interfere with normal things, and I figured I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about using an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even just thinking about using one can set me off a bit. It’s strange because logically I know, like, an elevator in an office building is probably safe. I’m not walking around thinking every modern elevator is actually dangerous or anything.\n\nBut when I’m standing in front of those older elevator doors—like the ones in my apartment building, or sometimes at work—it’s like my brain doesn’t care what I know. It immediately jumps to, “What if I get trapped in there?” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, like obviously people use them all day and nothing happens, but in the moment it feels very real. Part of me is aware I’m probably overreacting, but another part of me is already treating it like I’m about to step into something unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety or fear in situations other than using elevators?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can get nervous about normal stuff like anyone—work things, maybe meeting new people, that kind of thing—but it doesn’t feel like this. The elevator thing is very specific. It’s really the idea of being inside one, especially if it’s older or feels enclosed, and not being able to just get out.\n\nI’ve tried to think if there’s some bigger pattern, like small spaces in general or heights or crowds or something, but I don’t think so. I don’t walk around feeling scared of everything. It’s mainly elevators that seem to flip that switch for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in other small or enclosed spaces, like cars or tunnels?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, at least not in the same way. Like, I drive and ride in cars without really thinking about it. I mean, if traffic is bad or someone’s driving aggressively, I might be annoyed or tense, but it’s not that same trapped feeling.\n\nTunnels… I guess I don’t love being in a long tunnel, but I wouldn’t plan my day around avoiding one or anything. It doesn’t really stick in my mind afterward. With elevators, I’ll be thinking about it before I even get there, like where the stairs are, how many floors, whether I can make an excuse. With cars or tunnels, I don’t really do that.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed that it’s small spaces in general. It seems pretty specifically tied to elevators.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't think about elevators or worry about using them at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, there are definitely days I don’t think about them much at all. If I’m working from home or I’m just not going anywhere that involves an elevator, it’s not like it’s constantly in my head.\n\nBut once I know I might have to use one, especially at my apartment or at work with those older doors, then it pops up pretty fast. I’ll tell myself, like, this is probably safe, people use these all the time, it’s an office building or an apartment building, not some broken-down thing. But then I see the doors, and my mind just kind of treats it like, “Okay, you’re going to get trapped in there,” or “What if it drops?” And I know that sounds over the top when I’m standing here saying it, because part of me does know it’s probably fine. It’s just that in the moment, it doesn’t feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been afraid of elevators or is this something that started at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t always been afraid of them. It’s more recent than that. I’d say it started maybe around seven months ago, give or take.\n\nIt was shortly after I moved into my apartment—the one on the fifth floor—and there was this elevator scare that kind of set it off. Before that, I mean, I didn’t especially love elevators or anything, but I used them like a normal person. I didn’t really think about it much. Then after that happened, it’s like my brain started treating elevators differently, especially the one in my building. Since then it’s just sort of stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of elevators affect your daily plans and activities every day, or are there days when it doesn't interfere at all?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single day, no. There are plenty of days where it doesn’t really interfere, especially if I’m just going to places where I can take stairs easily or I’m not dealing with taller buildings.\n\nBut it has started affecting actual things, not just me feeling nervous. Like at work, I turned down this workshop assignment because it was in a high-rise building, and I knew I’d probably have to deal with the elevator situation. I kind of framed it like scheduling or fit or whatever, but honestly the elevator was a big reason.\n\nAnd with volunteering, I used to help this older student with computer stuff, like basic computer skills, and I stopped going to see him because his building basically requires using the elevator. I mean, technically maybe there are stairs, but realistically for where he lives and the way the building is set up, it felt like I couldn’t avoid it. So I just… stopped visiting, which I don’t feel great about.\n\nSo no, it’s not ruining every day from morning to night, but when elevators are part of the plan, it can change what I agree to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort when you were about to use an elevator or were already inside one?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It can come on really suddenly. Like I’ll be fine walking through the lobby, or at least mostly fine, and then as soon as I start actually heading toward the elevator, it’s like my body reacts before I’ve even fully thought about it.\n\nEspecially if I hear it make that clunking sound when the doors open—like that heavy mechanical sound—my chest tightens right away. My hands get sweaty, and there’s this rush of panic that hits me really fast. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, I’m afraid of this,” and then slowly get nervous. It’s more immediate than that. I’ll be standing there and suddenly I feel like I need to either back away or force myself through it.\n\nAnd sometimes it happens before I’m even inside. Just stepping toward it is enough. Which is frustrating, because part of me knows nothing has actually happened yet, but my body is already acting like something is wrong.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators the same for all elevators, or is it worse for certain types, like older ones or those in specific locations?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worse with certain elevators. I mean, if it’s a newer elevator in some office building, part of me can usually recognize, like, okay, this is probably inspected, people use it all day, it’s not actually dangerous. I don’t necessarily like it, but I can sometimes talk myself down a little.\n\nBut the older-looking ones are much worse, especially the ones with those heavy old doors, or the kind that make more noise when they open and close. Like in my apartment building, when I see those doors, my mind immediately goes to this image of being stuck inside, or the elevator suddenly dropping. And I know that sounds dramatic. I know, logically, an elevator in an office block or apartment building is probably safe. But when I’m standing there looking at it, especially if it looks worn or older, my brain doesn’t treat it like a normal safe thing. It treats it like I’m about to step into something that could trap me or fail somehow.\n\nSo, no, it’s not exactly equal across all elevators. Location matters too—my apartment building and sometimes work are worse, probably because I have to deal with them more and because the doors and the sounds just set off that whole thought pattern.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned an elevator scare that started your fear, can you tell me more about what happened during that incident?\n\nPatient: I’m actually not totally sure how to explain it, because I don’t have one clear memory where I can say, “That was the exact incident and everything started right there.” I know I’ve probably described it as an elevator scare before, but when I try to pin it down, it’s kind of fuzzy.\n\nI remember being scared in an elevator at some point, or at least having that feeling like something was wrong, but I don’t have a detailed, movie-like memory of what happened—like how long it lasted, who was there, what floor it was, or anything like that. It’s more like I remember the fear around it than the actual event, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t want to make up details just to give a better answer. What I do know is that elevators started feeling unsafe to me after that kind of experience, but I can’t really give you a clean story of, “the doors got stuck” or “it dropped” or anything specific like that. It’s more vague than I wish it was.\n\nDoctor: Is it always the sight or sound of the elevator that triggers your fear, or can it be triggered by other things as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the sight or sound, yeah, but I guess it’s not only that. The big one is still when I’m actually stepping toward it, like once I’ve decided I’m going to use it and I’m walking up to the doors. That can set it off even before anything happens.\n\nAnd the sound really does it too—when it clunks open or makes that heavy mechanical noise, my body reacts almost instantly. My chest tightens, my hands get sweaty, and there’s this sudden rush of panic before I’ve even had time to think it through. It feels like my reaction is faster than my thoughts, if that makes sense.\n\nSo it’s not always just seeing it from across the lobby. Sometimes I can see an elevator and be okay-ish if I’m not going near it. But as soon as I’m moving toward it, or I hear it open, that’s when it really hits.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators always triggered by the same things, like the sight or sound of specific elevators?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s usually the same general things, but the intensity depends on the elevator.
